<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Drug Interactions Involving P-Glycoprotein: Digoxin and Verapamil
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of drug interactions involving efflux transporters, specifically P-glycoprotein (P-gp), and its clinical consequences, particularly concerning digoxin toxicity. (理解药物相互作用的机制，特别是涉及外流转运蛋白P-糖蛋白(P-gp)，及其临床后果，特别是关于地高辛毒性的问题。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old male with a history of chronic atrial fibrillation and heart failure (NYHA Class II) has been stable on digoxin 0.25 mg daily for the past year. His baseline serum digoxin concentration was 0.9 ng/mL, which was considered therapeutic for his condition. Despite digoxin therapy, his ventricular rate remains poorly controlled during episodes of atrial fibrillation. His cardiologist decides to add a calcium channel blocker to improve rate control. One week after initiating verapamil 40 mg twice daily, the patient presents to the emergency department complaining of severe anorexia, nausea, vomiting, and visual disturbances described as yellowish-green halos around lights (xanthopsia). An ECG shows a junctional escape rhythm with a ventricular rate of 45 bpm. A serum digoxin level drawn shortly after presentation is markedly elevated at 3.4 ng/mL.

Which of the following pharmacological mechanisms BEST explains the patient's presentation?

(A) Verapamil competitively inhibits the renal tubular secretion of digoxin by competing for the same organic anion transporter (OAT) system.
(B) Verapamil induces the hepatic cytochrome P450 3A4 (CYP3A4) enzyme system, leading to decreased metabolism of digoxin and increased serum levels.
(C) Verapamil inhibits the P-glycoprotein (P-gp) efflux transporter, reducing the renal and biliary clearance of digoxin, thereby increasing its serum concentration.
(D) Verapamil increases the volume of distribution (Vd) of digoxin by displacing it from plasma protein binding sites, leading to a transient increase in free drug concentration.
(E) Verapamil enhances the enterohepatic recirculation of digoxin by inhibiting its metabolism in the gut, leading to increased absorption.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 72yo male, atrial fibrillation, heart failure, on digoxin. This sets the stage for potential drug interactions affecting digoxin pharmacokinetics.
- **Baseline Digoxin:** 0.25 mg daily, serum level 0.9 ng/mL (therapeutic). Establishes the patient's usual digoxin dose and concentration.
- **New Medication:** Verapamil added for rate control. Verapamil is a known drug interaction agent.
- **Symptoms:** Anorexia, nausea, vomiting, xanthopsia (yellowish-green halos), junctional escape rhythm (bradycardia). These are classic signs and symptoms of digoxin toxicity.
- **Elevated Digoxin Level:** Serum digoxin level increased dramatically to 3.4 ng/mL (toxic range is typically >2.0 ng/mL). Confirms digoxin toxicity.
- **Timing:** Symptoms and elevated level occurred one week after starting verapamil. Suggests a drug interaction between digoxin and verapamil.
- **Question:** Asks for the *mechanism* explaining the toxicity.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Digoxin Pharmacokinetics:** Digoxin is primarily eliminated by renal excretion. A significant portion of this renal excretion involves active tubular secretion mediated by the P-glycoprotein (P-gp) efflux transporter (also known as MDR1 or ABCB1) located on the apical membrane of proximal tubule cells. P-gp pumps digoxin from the tubular cells back into the tubular lumen, preventing its reabsorption and facilitating its excretion in the urine. Digoxin also undergoes some biliary excretion, which is also mediated by P-gp.
- **Verapamil Pharmacokinetics:** Verapamil is a potent inhibitor of P-gp.
- **Interaction Mechanism:** When verapamil is co-administered with digoxin, it inhibits P-gp. This inhibition reduces the active tubular secretion of digoxin into the urine and decreases its biliary excretion. Consequently, less digoxin is cleared from the body, leading to a significant increase in serum digoxin concentrations.
- **Clinical Consequence:** The increased serum digoxin level overwhelms the therapeutic range and causes toxicity, manifesting as the symptoms described in the vignette (GI upset, visual disturbances, bradyarrhythmias).
- **Kaplan/First Aid Relevance:** P-gp is a major efflux transporter involved in the pharmacokinetics of many drugs. Its inhibition is a common cause of drug interactions, leading to increased plasma concentrations of substrates like digoxin. Digoxin's renal clearance is heavily dependent on P-gp. Verapamil, amiodarone, and quinidine are classic potent P-gp inhibitors known to significantly increase digoxin levels.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Verapamil does *not* primarily inhibit digoxin secretion via the organic anion transporter (OAT) system. While OATs are involved in renal secretion of some drugs, digoxin's primary renal secretion mechanism is P-gp. Probenecid is a classic OAT inhibitor that can increase digoxin levels, but verapamil's main effect is via P-gp inhibition. This distractor tests the confusion between different renal transporter systems.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Verapamil is an inhibitor, not an inducer, of CYP3A4. While CYP3A4 does play a minor role in digoxin metabolism, its inhibition by verapamil would *decrease* digoxin metabolism, leading to *increased* levels, but this is not the *primary* mechanism. The major interaction is via P-gp inhibition. This distractor tests the confusion between enzyme induction/inhibition and transporter inhibition.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Verapamil does not significantly displace digoxin from plasma protein binding sites to a clinically relevant extent that would cause such a dramatic increase in serum levels and toxicity. Digoxin is moderately bound to plasma proteins (~25%), and displacement effects are usually less significant than transporter or metabolic interactions. This distractor tests the concept of protein binding displacement.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Verapamil does not inhibit digoxin metabolism in the gut or significantly enhance its enterohepatic recirculation. Digoxin absorption is generally good, and enterohepatic recirculation is not a major factor in its overall elimination. The primary issue is reduced *clearance* (renal and biliary) due to P-gp inhibition, not increased *absorption*. This distractor tests the concept of enterohepatic recirculation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** P-glycoprotein (P-gp) is a critical efflux transporter involved in the renal and biliary clearance of digoxin. Potent P-gp inhibitors like verapamil, amiodarone, and quinidine significantly increase digoxin serum levels, potentially leading to toxicity. Always reduce digoxin dose by ~50% when starting these interacting drugs.
- **Differentials:** Compare P-gp inhibition (verapamil, amiodarone, quinidine) with CYP3A4 inhibition (e.g., ritonavir, erythromycin - minor effect on digoxin) or renal secretion inhibition via OATs (probenecid). Understand that P-gp inhibition is the *dominant* mechanism for the interaction between digoxin and verapamil/amiodarone/quinidine.

---
